> 64	 *
> 65	 * Antes a camera tinha 1 grau de liberdade de 3: so `z` era interpolado por keyframes, e `x`/`y`
> 66	 * eram um seno e um cosseno do tempo (a "deriva"). Dava dolly e locked-off, e mais nada —
> 67	 * travelling, grua e reenquadramento nao existiam. A auditoria da regua de cinema marcou isso
> 68	 * como lacuna L10, e o Sovereign pediu pelo nome: *"criar uma trilha para seguir com a camera
> 69	 * tecnicamente igual nos software que usa os eixos para movimentar as cameras"*.
> 70	 *
> 71	 * O keyframe aceita as DUAS formas, e a normalizacao e aqui:
> 72	 *     [t, z]           -> legado, x=0 y=0 (toda cena antiga continua rendendo igual)
> 73	 *     [t, x, y, z]     -> trilha: os tres eixos autorados, como em qualquer software de 3D
> 74	 *
> 75	 * A deriva senoidal continua existindo e e ADITIVA — ela e respiro de camera na mao, nao
> 76	 * direcao. Numa cena com trilha autorada, zere `driftX`/`driftY` se quiser o movimento limpo.
> 77	 */
> 78	const CAM = (spec.camera?.keyframes
> 79	  ?? [[0, spec.camera?.fromZ ?? 0], [D, spec.camera?.toZ ?? 380]]
> 80	).map((k) => (k.length >= 4 ? [k[0], k[1], k[2], k[3]] : [k[0], 0, 0, k[1]]));
> 81	const DRIFT_X = spec.camera?.driftX ?? 24;
> 82	const DRIFT_Y = spec.camera?.driftY ?? 13;
> 83	
> 84	const PLANES = spec.planes ?? {};
> 85	if (!Object.keys(PLANES).length) { console.error("spec.planes vazio — MONTAR sem Z declarado nao existe (era o defeito do EP06)."); process.exit(2); }
> 86	
> 87	const FOCUS = {
> 88	  stations: spec.focus?.stations ?? [[0, Object.keys(PLANES)[0]]],
> 89	  k: spec.focus?.k ?? 80,
> 90	  maxBlur: spec.focus?.maxBlur ?? 11,
> 91	  // `motivated: true` troca a metrica de dof por distancia OPTICA (a camera entra na conta).
> 92	  // Fica opt-in porque muda o valor de `k`: no modo antigo k e "px de Z por px de blur"; no
> 93	  // motivado k e um ganho adimensional, tipicamente 40-120. Ligar sem re-tunar k reprova no
> 94	  // gate de ">=3 planos de desfoque distintos" — e reprovar em silencio e o defeito que a
> 95	  // linhagem toda combate.
> 96	  motivated: spec.focus?.motivated === true,
> 97	};
> 98	if (FOCUS.motivated && spec.focus?.k == null) {
> 99	  console.error("focus.motivated exige focus.k explicito — o k do modo antigo nao transfere (unidade diferente).");
> 100	  process.exit(2);
> 101	}
> 102	for (const [, pid] of FOCUS.stations) {
> 103	  if (!(pid in PLANES)) { console.error(`focus.stations aponta plano inexistente: ${pid}`); process.exit(2); }
> 104	}
> 105	
> 106	const LAYERS = spec.layers ?? [];
> 107	for (const l of LAYERS) {
> 108	  if (!(l.plane in PLANES)) { console.error(`layer ${l.id ?? "?"} aponta plano inexistente: ${l.plane}`); process.exit(2); }
> 109	}
> 110	const HUD = spec.hud ?? {};
> 111	const ATMO = spec.atmosphere ?? { weave: true, horizon: true, nebulae: [] };
> 112	
> 113	const fontB64 = readFileSync(
> 114	  "/home/gcrUX/htdocs/constellation-orchestra/scripts/hyperframes/templates/fonts/Montserrat-variable.ttf",
> 115	).toString("base64");
> 116	
> 117	const esc = (s) => String(s ?? "")
> 118	  .replace(/&/g, "&amp;").replace(/</g, "&lt;").replace(/>/g, "&gt;")
> 119	  .replace(/"/g, "&quot;").replace(/'/g, "&#39;");
> 120	const srcUrl = (p) => (p && p.startsWith("/") ? `file://${p}` : p ?? "");
> 121	const num = (v, d = 0) => Number.isFinite(Number(v)) ? Number(v) : d;
> 122	
> 123	// ── ANCORAGEM UNIFORME (achado do Diretor de Arte, 2026-08-11): TODA camada e posicionada pelo
> 124	//    seu CENTRO — e o centro e o que as 4 lentes escrevem nas Partituras. Os kinds de largura
> 125	//    intrinseca (o conteudo dita a caixa) nao tem w/h para subtrair, entao sao centrados por
> 126	//    translate(-50%,-50%), que o runtime PREFIXA em todo transform que escrever neles.
> 127	//    Antes desta correcao metade das camadas nascia deslocada de meia-caixa. ────────────────────
> 128	const CENTER_KINDS = new Set(["cutout", "chip", "badge", "counter", "stamp"]);
> 129	
> 130	// ── VOC-ART-M-005 (Diretor de Arte): `style` por camada nos kinds de texto. Destrava o piso
> 131	//    tipografico do DoP, o token da casa "gold so para VALOR" e o contador sem tremor de largura
> 132	//    (numeral tabular). Sem isso, tipo e cor eram constantes do motor — decisao de arte na mao
> 133	//    do engenheiro. ────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
> 134	function styleAttr(s) {
> 135	  if (!s) return "";
> 136	  const css = [];
> 137	  if (s.size) css.push(`font-size:${num(s.size, 16)}px`);
> 138	  if (s.color) css.push(`color:${esc(s.color)};-webkit-text-fill-color:${esc(s.color)};background:none`);
> 139	  if (s.weight) css.push(`font-weight:${num(s.weight, 700)}`);
> 140	  if (s.tracking) css.push(`letter-spacing:${s.tracking}`);
> 141	  if (s.numerals === "tabular") css.push("font-variant-numeric:tabular-nums;font-feature-settings:'tnum' 1");
> 142	  return css.length ? ` style="${css.join(";")}"` : "";
> 143	}
> 144	
> 145	// ── HTML por kind. A FOLHA de toda camada leva data-leaf="<i>" — e nela (nunca no .pl)
> 146	//    que o runtime escreve opacity/filter. ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────
> 147	function layerHtml(l, i) {
> 148	  const x = num(l.x), y = num(l.y), w = num(l.w, 300), h = num(l.h, 200);
> 149	  const pos = `left:${x - w / 2}px;top:${y - h / 2}px;width:${w}px;height:${h}px`;
> 150	  switch (l.kind) {
> 151	    case "slab": {
> 152	      const isVideo = l.src?.endsWith(".mp4") || l.src?.endsWith(".webm") || l.media === "video";
> 153	      const media = isVideo
> 154	        ? `<video src="${esc(srcUrl(l.src))}" muted playsinline preload="auto" data-vid="${i}"></video>`
> 155	        : `<img src="${esc(srcUrl(l.src))}" alt="">`;
> 156	      // VOC-ART-M-004: `plain` = lamina NUA (sem sheen/rim/borda/opacity .95). Existe para marca de
> 157	      // terceiro nao vestir o figurino da casa — chrome de UI capturado tem que aparecer INTACTO.
> 158	      const plain = l.plain === true;
> 159	      return `<div class="slab${plain ? " plain" : ""}" data-leaf="${i}" style="${pos}">
> 160	        ${media}${plain ? "" : `<div class="sheen"></div><div class="rim"></div>`}
> 161	        ${l.tag ? `<div class="tag">${esc(l.tag)}</div>` : ""}</div>`;
> 162	    }
> 163	    case "cutout":
> 164	      // `glow:false` desliga o halo ciano — marca de terceiro nunca pode receber pigmento da casa.
> 165	      return `<div class="cutwrap${l.glow === false ? " noglow" : ""}" data-leaf="${i}" style="left:${x}px;top:${y}px">
> 166	        <img src="${esc(srcUrl(l.src))}" alt="" style="width:${w}px;height:auto"></div>`;
> 167	    case "chip":
> 168	      return `<div class="chip" data-leaf="${i}" style="left:${x}px;top:${y}px">
> 169	        ${l.iconPath ? `<div class="mk"><svg viewBox="0 0 24 24" fill="none" stroke="#06121f" stroke-width="2.5"><path d="${esc(l.iconPath)}"/></svg></div>` : ""}
> 170	        <div>${l.t1 ? `<div class="t1"${styleAttr(l.style)}>${esc(l.t1)}</div>` : ""}${l.t2 ? `<div class="t2">${esc(l.t2)}</div>` : ""}</div></div>`;
> 171	    case "badge":
> 172	      return `<div class="badge" data-leaf="${i}" style="left:${x}px;top:${y}px">
> 173	        <svg viewBox="0 0 24 24">${l.iconSvg ?? '<rect x="3" y="5" width="18" height="14" rx="2"/><path d="M3 15l5-5 4 4 3-3 6 6"/>'}</svg></div>`;
> 174	    case "data": {
> 175	      const cols = (l.series ?? []).map((s, bi) => `
> 176	        <div class="col" data-bar="${i}:${bi}" style="height:0px;width:${num(l.colWidth, 126)}px">
> 177	          <div class="glow" style="background:${esc(s.glow ?? "#7c48ff")}"></div>
> 178	          <div class="body" style="background:${esc(s.fill ?? "linear-gradient(180deg,#c4b5fd,#6d28d9)")}"></div>
> 179	          <div class="blab"><div class="n" style="color:${esc(s.color ?? "#ccbcff")}">${esc(s.label)}</div><div class="k">${esc(s.name ?? "")}</div></div>
> 180	        </div>`).join("");
> 181	      return `<div class="dataviz" data-leaf="${i}" style="left:${x - w / 2}px;top:${y - h / 2}px;width:${w}px">
> 182	        ${l.title ? `<div class="dttl">${esc(l.title)}</div>` : ""}
> 183	        <div class="plot" style="height:${num(l.plotH, 320)}px;gap:${num(l.gap, 120)}px">${cols}</div>
> 184	        ${l.caption ? `<div class="dcap">${esc(l.caption)}</div>` : ""}</div>`;
> 185	    }
> 186	    case "splitbar":
> 187	      return `<div class="splitbar" data-leaf="${i}" style="left:${x - w / 2}px;top:${y - h / 2}px;width:${w}px">
> 188	        ${l.title ? `<div class="dttl">${esc(l.title)}</div>` : ""}
> 189	        <div class="sbtrack" style="height:${num(l.barH, 84)}px">
> 190	          <div class="sbL" data-sb="${i}:L"><span class="sbv">${esc(l.left?.label ?? "")}</span><span class="sbk">${esc(l.left?.name ?? "")}</span></div>
> 191	          <div class="sbgap" data-sb="${i}:G"></div>
> 192	          <div class="sbR" data-sb="${i}:R"><span class="sbv">${esc(l.right?.label ?? "")}</span><span class="sbk">${esc(l.right?.name ?? "")}</span></div>
> 193	        </div>
> 194	        ${l.caption ? `<div class="dcap">${esc(l.caption)}</div>` : ""}</div>`;
> 195	    case "timeline": {
> 196	      const rows = (l.rows ?? []).map((r, ri) => {
> 197	        const ticks = (r.marks ?? []).map((m, mi) =>
> 198	          `<div class="tlmark" data-tl="${i}:${ri}:${mi}" style="left:${(m * 100).toFixed(2)}%"></div>`).join("");
> 199	        return `<div class="tlrow"><div class="tllab">${esc(r.name ?? "")}</div>
> 200	          <div class="tlbar" style="height:${num(l.barH, 14)}px">${ticks}</div>
> 201	          <div class="tlcount" data-tlc="${i}:${ri}">${esc(r.countLabel ?? "")}</div></div>`;
> 202	      }).join("");
> 203	      return `<div class="timeline" data-leaf="${i}" style="left:${x - w / 2}px;top:${y - h / 2}px;width:${w}px">
> 204	        ${l.title ? `<div class="dttl">${esc(l.title)}</div>` : ""}${rows}
> 205	        ${l.caption ? `<div class="dcap">${esc(l.caption)}</div>` : ""}</div>`;
> 206	    }
> 207	    case "counter":
> 208	      // numeral tabular por default: numero que cresce sem tremer de largura (PV-1 do Dramaturgo).
> 209	      return `<div class="counter" data-leaf="${i}" style="left:${x}px;top:${y}px">
> 210	        <div class="cv" data-cv="${i}"${styleAttr({ numerals: "tabular", ...(l.style ?? {}) })}>&nbsp;</div>
> 211	        ${l.name ? `<div class="ck">${esc(l.name)}</div>` : ""}</div>`;
> 212	    case "stamp":
> 213	      return `<div class="stamp" data-leaf="${i}" style="left:${x}px;top:${y}px">
> 214	        <div class="sv"${styleAttr(l.style)}>${esc(l.text ?? "")}</div>
> 215	        ${l.source ? `<div class="ssrc">${esc(l.source)}</div>` : ""}</div>`;
> 216	    default:
> 217	      console.error(`kind desconhecido: ${l.kind} (layer ${l.id ?? i}) — Pedido de Vocabulario, nao improviso.`);
> 218	      process.exit(2);
> 219	  }
> 220	}
> 221	
> 222	// agrupa camadas por plano — um .pl por plano nomeado, na ordem do spec.planes
> 223	const byPlane = {};
> 224	LAYERS.forEach((l, i) => { (byPlane[l.plane] ??= []).push([l, i]); });
> 225	const planesHtml = Object.keys(PLANES).map((pid) => {
> 226	  const inner = (byPlane[pid] ?? []).map(([l, i]) => layerHtml(l, i)).join("\n");
> 227	  const extra = pid === (ATMO.horizonPlane ?? Object.keys(PLANES)[0]) && ATMO.horizon
> 228	    ? `${(ATMO.nebulae ?? []).map((n) => `<div class="neb" style="left:${n.x}px;top:${n.y}px;width:${n.w}px;height:${n.h}px;background:${esc(n.color)}"></div>`).join("")}<div id="horizon"></div>`
> 229	    : "";
> 230	  return `<div class="pl" data-plane="${esc(pid)}">${extra}${inner}</div>`;
> 231	}).join("\n");
> 232	
> 233	// runtime config — so o que o seek(t) precisa
> 234	const RT = {
> 235	  W, H, D, P: PERSP, CAM, DRIFT_X, DRIFT_Y,
> 236	  planes: PLANES,
> 237	  focus: FOCUS,
> 238	  layers: LAYERS.map((l, i) => ({
> 239	    i, kind: l.kind, plane: l.plane, z: PLANES[l.plane], ctr: CENTER_KINDS.has(l.kind),
> 240	    in: l.in ?? [0.1, 0.9], out: l.out ?? [Math.max(0, D - 0.9), D - 0.2],
> 241	    rotY: l.rotY ?? null, rotZ: num(l.rotZ, 0), approach: num(l.approach, 0),
> 242	    spring: l.spring ?? (l.kind === "badge" || l.kind === "chip" || l.kind === "stamp" || l.kind === "cutout"),
> 243	    slideX: num(l.slideX, 0), slideY: num(l.slideY, l.kind === "chip" ? 34 : 0),
> 244	    bars: l.kind === "data" ? (l.series ?? []).map((s, bi) => ({ bi, v: num(s.value), max: Math.max(...(l.series ?? []).map((q) => num(q.value)), 1), grow: s.grow ?? [num(l.growFrom, 1.0) + bi * 0.2, num(l.growFrom, 1.0) + 1.5 + bi * 0.2], barMax: num(l.barMax, 230) })) : null,
> 245	    split: l.kind === "splitbar" ? { lv: num(l.left?.value, 50), rv: num(l.right?.value, 50), at: l.splitAt ?? [1.0, 2.2] } : null,
> 246	    tl: l.kind === "timeline" ? (l.rows ?? []).map((r, ri) => ({ ri, marks: (r.marks ?? []).length, from: num(r.from, 1.0), step: num(r.step, 0.35) })) : null,
> 247	    cnt: l.kind === "counter" ? { from: num(l.from), to: num(l.to), win: l.countWin ?? [0.6, 2.6], prefix: l.prefix ?? "", suffix: l.suffix ?? "", decimals: num(l.decimals, 0) } : null,
> 248	  })),
> 249	  hud: {

> 258	const scrimCss = RT.hud.scrim === "bottom"

> 261	
> 262	const html = `<!doctype html><html lang="pt-BR"><head><meta charset="utf-8">
> 263	<title>montar-v2 ${esc(spec.cena ?? "")}</title>
> 264	<style>
> 265	@font-face{font-family:'Mont';src:url(data:font/ttf;base64,${fontB64}) format('truetype');font-weight:100 900;font-display:block}
> 266	*{margin:0;padding:0;box-sizing:border-box}
> 267	html,body{width:${W}px;height:${H}px;overflow:hidden;background:#04050a}

> 269	#wash{position:absolute;inset:0;
> 270	  background:
> 271	    radial-gradient(110% 80% at 50% 2%, rgba(78,40,168,.34), transparent 60%),
> 272	    radial-gradient(70% 46% at 50% 104%, rgba(0,150,190,.22), transparent 64%),

> 274	    linear-gradient(#05060c,#03030a 58%,#070512)}
> 275	#weave{position:absolute;left:50%;top:50%;width:1680px;height:1000px;margin-left:-840px;margin-top:-500px;
> 276	  background:

> 283	#cam{position:absolute;inset:0;transform-style:preserve-3d;will-change:transform}
> 284	/* .pl: GEOMETRIA pura. will-change:transform e NADA MAIS (armadilha 1 do header). */
> 285	.pl{position:absolute;left:50%;top:50%;transform-style:preserve-3d;will-change:transform}
> 286	/* coordenadas RELATIVAS AO CENTRO do plano (o .pl e 0x0) — nada de % aqui */
> 287	#horizon{position:absolute;left:-1700px;top:${num(ATMO.horizonY, 380)}px;width:3400px;height:5px;border-radius:50%;
> 288	  background:linear-gradient(90deg,transparent,rgba(0,220,255,.6) 22%,rgba(150,90,255,.62) 62%,transparent);
> 289	  filter:blur(12px)}

> 291	.slab{position:absolute;border-radius:32px;overflow:hidden;background:#080b14;

> 299	  box-shadow:inset 0 0 70px rgba(124,72,255,.34), inset 0 1px 0 rgba(255,255,255,.20)}

> 301	  color:#cdeeff;opacity:.85;font-weight:600;text-shadow:0 2px 10px #000}
> 302	/* ancoragem por CENTRO nos kinds de largura intrinseca — o mesmo contrato dos kinds com w/h */

> 317	.chip .t2{font-size:13px;opacity:.75;margin-top:3px}
> 318	.badge{position:absolute;width:62px;height:62px;border-radius:50%;
> 319	  background:rgba(18,10,38,.92);border:2px solid rgba(172,126,255,.9);

> 323	.dataviz,.splitbar,.timeline{position:absolute;will-change:transform,filter,opacity}
> 324	.dttl{text-align:center;font-size:16px;font-weight:700;letter-spacing:.2em;text-transform:uppercase;
> 325	  color:#9fd8ff;margin-bottom:20px}
> 326	.plot{display:flex;align-items:flex-end;justify-content:center}
> 327	.col{position:relative;border-radius:20px 20px 5px 5px}
> 328	.col .glow{position:absolute;inset:-16px -12px -6px;border-radius:30px;filter:blur(22px);opacity:.9}
> 329	.col .body{position:absolute;inset:0;border-radius:20px 20px 5px 5px}
> 330	.blab{position:absolute;left:50%;bottom:100%;margin-bottom:14px;transform:translateX(-50%);
> 331	  text-align:center;white-space:nowrap;height:66px}
> 332	.blab .n{font-size:44px;font-weight:800;letter-spacing:-.025em;line-height:1}
> 333	.blab .k{font-size:15px;font-weight:700;letter-spacing:.18em;text-transform:uppercase;opacity:.9;margin-top:7px}

> 445	      return new Promise(function(res){
> 446	        var done=false;

> 448	        v.addEventListener('seeked',ok,{once:true});

> 451	      });
> 452	    });
> 453	    return Promise.all(ps);

> 456	  async function seek(t){
> 457	    if(t<0)t=0; if(t>D)t=D;
> 458	    var cam3=camAt(t), cz=cam3.z, fz=focusZ(t);
> 459	    // trilha autorada + respiro senoidal (aditivo). Trilha zerada = comportamento antigo.
> 460	    var dx=cam3.x+Math.sin(t*0.40)*C.DRIFT_X, dy=cam3.y+Math.cos(t*0.31)*C.DRIFT_Y;
> 461	    cam.style.transform='translate3d('+dx.toFixed(2)+'px,'+dy.toFixed(2)+'px,'+cz.toFixed(1)+'px)';
> 462	
> 463	    var zs=[]; for(var pk in C.planes) zs.push(C.planes[pk]);
> 464	    var zBack=Math.min.apply(null,zs);
> 465	    if(weaveEl){
> 466	      var wEff=cz+zBack, wS=P/(P-wEff);
> 467	      weaveEl.style.transform='translate('+(dx*wS).toFixed(2)+'px,'+(dy*wS).toFixed(2)+'px) scale('+wS.toFixed(4)+')';
> 468	    }
> 469	    for(var pk2 in C.planes){
> 470	      var pel=planeEls[pk2];
> 471	      if(pel) pel.style.transform='translate3d(-50%,-50%,'+C.planes[pk2]+'px)';
> 472	    }
> 473	    // FOCO MOTIVADO — a camera entra na conta.
> 474	    // Antes: dof(z)=|z-fz|/k, so a distancia em Z de CENA. cz estava aqui no escopo, tres
> 475	    // linhas acima, e nunca era usado — entao a camera avancava 380px no dolly e a profundidade
> 476	    // de campo nao mudava um pixel. Opticamente impossivel, e o olho percebe sem saber nomear.
> 477	    // Agora a metrica e a distancia OPTICA ate a camera, como numa lente de verdade: o que esta
> 478	    // perto do plano focal fica nitido, e o desfoque cresce mais rapido no que esta perto da
> 479	    // camera do que no que esta longe — que e a assimetria que faz o quadro parecer fotografado.
> 480	    // cz cai para 1 quando a formula degenera (camera no plano de projecao), preservando o
> 481	    // comportamento antigo em vez de estourar em Infinity.
> 482	    function dof(z){
> 483	      if(!C.focus.motivated) return clamp(Math.abs(z-fz)/C.focus.k,0,C.focus.maxBlur);
> 484	      var dz=P-(cz+z), df=P-(cz+fz);
> 485	      if(dz<=1||df<=1) return C.focus.maxBlur;
> 486	      return clamp(C.focus.k*Math.abs(1/df-1/dz)*P,0,C.focus.maxBlur);
> 487	    }
> 488	
> 489	    var gIn=smooth(seg(t,0,C.fades.in)), gOut=smooth(seg(t,D-C.fades.out,D));

> 492	    for(var li=0;li<C.layers.length;li++){

> 510	        if(L.slideY) parts.push('translateY('+((1-sIn)*L.slideY).toFixed(1)+'px)');
> 511	        if(L.kind==='badge'||L.kind==='stamp') parts.push('scale('+sIn.toFixed(3)+')');
> 512	        tr=parts.join(' ');
> 513	      } else if(L.approach){
> 514	        tr='translateZ('+((1-ease(seg(t,L.in[0],L.in[1])))*-L.approach).toFixed(1)+'px)';
> 515	      }
> 516	      // A centragem PREFIXA todo transform (e e escrita mesmo quando nao ha animacao) — senao o
> 517	      // primeiro transform do runtime apagaria o translate(-50%,-50%) do CSS e a camada saltaria
> 518	      // meia-caixa no meio da cena.
> 519	      if(L.ctr) el.style.transform='translate(-50%,-50%) '+tr;
> 520	      else if(tr) el.style.transform=tr;
> 521	
> 522	      if(L.bars){
> 523	        for(var b=0;b<L.bars.length;b++){

> 528	          bel.style.height=(BR.barMax*(BR.v/BR.max)*Math.max(grow,0)).toFixed(1)+'px';
> 529	          bel.style.opacity=String(ease(seg(t,BR.grow[0]-0.1,BR.grow[0]+0.6)));
> 530	        }
> 531	      }

> 585	  function guardImages(cb){

> 587	    var pend=imgs.length, bad=[];

> 595	      }

## 🤖 Claude *(22:29:30)*

<details>
<summary>💭 Thinking Process</summary>

Looking at the engine's camera implementation, the `CAM` normalization handles the `[t,x,y,z]` format correctly, and the `camAt()` function uses smoothstep interpolation between keyframes, which means the camera comes to a complete stop at each keyframe—useful for deliberate pauses. The camera's x/y translation moves the entire `#cam` wrapper containing all planes, though the thought cuts off mid-explanation about how this translation works.

Now I'm working through the perspective math: the `#cam` element has `translate3d(dx, dy, cz)` applied, and since `#stage` has perspective set, the child planes at `translate3d(-50%, -50%, z_plane)` compose into a world position of `(dx-50%, dy-50%, cz+z_plane)`. The perspective projection then scales both x/y and z by the factor `P/(P - z_eff)` where `z_eff` is the effective depth. So the camera's x/y translation actually behaves as a parallel shift in screen-space, scaled by the perspective factor based on the plane's depth.

Since planes are positioned at negative z values (like -1150 to -4200), increasing the camera's z coordinate (from 0 to 1320) makes `z_eff` less negative, which increases the scale factor and makes objects appear larger—that's the dolly-in effect. At the `cifra` plane's z=-1150 with camera z=760 at t=15.34, the effective depth is -390, giving a scale of about 0.769, so the counter renders at roughly 77% of its layout size. The counter at that scale would be about 44 pixels tall on a phone screen—readable but not dominant. The real lever here is the camera z-position, which scales everything globally, but since the cifra plane is frontmost, it gains scale fastest as I push the camera back, letting me make those numbers truly dominate the composition.

I'm working through the math on how scale changes across different camera z values, computing the effective depth and resulting magnification for the cifra plane to find the sweet spot where it visually takes over.

Now I'm considering whether to enable the motivated focus mode, which would give camera-dependent depth-of-field calculations but requires re-tuning the blur coefficient. The spec doesn't explicitly use it, so I need to weigh whether the added complexity is worth the visual benefit.

The key insight is that motivated focus creates a real optical effect: as the camera pushes in at 15.34s, the background naturally separates more, making the subject dominate through selective blur. This is already implemented in the engine and uses the same blur filter that's already running, so there's no performance cost beyond what's already there — the blur radius just changes based on camera distance. The performance impact scales with the leaf cutout areas and blur radius, which I can keep reasonable at 10-12 pixels.

The tricky part is tuning the depth-of-field calculation correctly so the frame doesn't accidentally max out the blur. I'm working through the math now: at the 15.34s mark with the camera at z=760 and focus on the cifra at z=-1150, I need to compute how the depth difference translates to blur amount across different planes in the scene.

For the host plane at z=-2300, a k value around 25.7 gives roughly 8px of blur, which is lower than the typical 40-120 range but makes sense given how spread out the z-coordinates are in this scene. The real concern is the grid plane at z=-4200—with that same k value, the blur calculation pushes toward the maximum, so I need to verify the clamping behavior doesn't cause unwanted artifacts. I found the issue — the cutout is rendering with `width: 300` (the default) and `height: auto`. For the host image (603×1787 pixels, aspect ratio 0.337), the spec says height should be 868 but doesn't specify width, so it defaults to 300, which actually produces a rendered height around 890 pixels. That's suspiciously close to the intended 868, almost like whoever set this up calculated the width from the height they wanted.

At camera zoom zero, the scale factor is 0.361, so the host renders at 321 pixels tall on screen — about 44.6% of the 720-pixel frame height, which explains the floating sticker appearance. The host2 image (677×1645, aspect 0.4116) also defaults to width 300, giving a layout height of 729 pixels, which scales down to 263 pixels at the same camera position. Both images sit on the same plane but render at different heights (321 vs 263) with no scale relationship between them — that's exactly what the defect describes. The root cause is the broken `h` field being ignored in favor of `w`, which is an engine issue that needs fixing in the vocabulary request.

The `h` parameter is dead code for cutout elements; only `w` is used, while `layerHtml` computes `h` but never passes it through. This same pattern repeats for chip, badge, counter, and stamp elements — all intrinsic-width kinds that ignore height. The fix is straightforward: cutout should honor the `h` field so that when two people stand on the same floor, their height ratio stays consistent regardless of their PNG aspect ratios. Right now the author can only control width, making it impossible to maintain proper scale relationships between figures.

I'm wrestling with where this responsibility belongs. The task asks for concrete patchLayers changes — time in/out values plus field modifications — but if I don't fix the width calculation and leave the scale relationship broken, my camera adjustments will just amplify the problem. I need to think through the ownership pragmatically. Now I'm calculating the actual rendered dimensions for each element with the default width of 300. The host layer renders at 890 pixels tall, host2 at 729, but the microphone and mug are both coming out at 300×300 when they should be much taller based on their specifications — the mic should be around 623 pixels and the mug around 280, so they're rendering at roughly half their intended size.

This creates a major visual problem: the microphone and mug end up nearly identical in size when the mic should be more than twice as tall. That's a clear scaling defect. Let me recalculate the correct width values needed to hit the intended heights — host needs about 293 (already close at 300), host2 needs 347, the mic needs 623, and the mug needs 280.

The real issue is that with both presenters at width 300, their height ratio becomes 22% instead of the intended 3%, making them look like an adult and teenager rather than two similar-sized people. Now I'm moving on to define the camera track and target apparent scales for the animation phases.

For the opening shot, I want the widest possible frame to establish the full scene before everything tightens up. The two nebula blobs render at roughly 146 and 123 pixels wide, positioned near the center of the 1720-pixel frame. This means the entire atmosphere occupies only about 400 pixels of width—it's a small island in the middle of an anamorphic canvas. Since this is fundamentally about how light and atmosphere are rendered, it falls squarely in my domain.

I can see a few potential fixes: moving the grid plane much closer to the camera would expand the atmosphere, though that's Encenador's responsibility and would shift the parallax rate. Or I could enlarge the nebulae themselves.

The nebulae parameters—position, size, color—are part of the atmosphere spec, which is my field since atmosphere defines where light comes from. The weave dimensions are hardcoded at 1680×1000 in the template, but I should request atmosphere parameters for weave width and height, or scale the weave to match the canvas. Now I'm calculating what nebula sizes would be needed to fill the frame properly—at the grid's depth, I need to work backward from the desired screen size to find the layout dimensions. astrophysically catastrophic. A 70px blur on a 27 MP layer would absolutely tank performance — that's exactly the kind of rasterization cost that kills frame rates. I'm weighing the trade-off: spreading four moderately-sized nebulae across the frame would add roughly 3.4 megapixels of blur work, translating to 56–112 milliseconds per frame or about 1–2 minutes to the total 7-minute render. It's a measurable cost, but it would fill the emptiest parts of the composition across the entire sequence, so I think it's worth pursuing—though I need to measure the actual impact and have a cheaper fallback ready.

The wash layer itself is already a set of radial gradients that could theoretically provide ambient fill for free, but it's baked into the template CSS and tied to design tokens, so that's not flexible. There's also a simpler option: if the nebulae were larger and didn't rely so heavily on the blur filter to diffuse, they'd read as atmospheric light without the performance hit—but that's a CSS constraint I can't easily change. Since the z-depth spread between the presenters and the background elements is too large to work within the staging constraints, I'm settling on a compromise: render the presenters at about 76-80% of frame height with the feet clipped by 5-10%, which gives generous headroom even if the head sits lower in the 2.39:1 aspect ratio. This is actually a legitimate documentary look—figures in a void—rather than trying to force a tighter figure-in-frame composition that the geometry won't support.

Now I'm working through the specific math: at a camera z-depth of 1540, the scale factor is about 0.62, which puts a 551-pixel figure height at 76.5% of the 720-pixel frame. Looking at the counter data across two different implementations: the ATUAL version shows the number still animating at 16.55 seconds with a size of 531×71 pixels, then reaching the full 100 million mark by 22.61 seconds at 671×83 pixels. The PATCH version already displays the complete 100 million value at 16.55 seconds but at a much larger 1200×148 pixel dimension, which represents about 70% of the container width.

During the visible lifespan of the counter, PATCH maintains roughly 1.74× the linear size and 3× the area compared to ATUAL at their respective peaks. The critical difference is at the moment "milhões" is spoken—ATUAL still shows an incomplete rolling number while PATCH displays the correct landed value at significantly larger dimensions.

Now examining the clipping issues: PATCH has a clipping flag at 3.90 seconds where the host container is cut off, with coordinates indicating the element extends beyond the visible bounds. The mug's dimensions shift with the corrected width, but the core issue remains whether a partially cut object reads as intentional grounding or a framing problem. Moving through the timeline: the empty frame at t=12.23 works as designed, then the chips enter and exit on schedule—chip1 and chip2 appear first, chip3 joins briefly, and by t=15.34 all three are visible with chip1 positioned off-screen to the left.

At the apex around t=16.55, the composition tightens: chip1 stays clipped on the left edge, chip2 centers in the frame, chip3 pushes toward the right margin with about 53 pixels of breathing room, and the counter element stretches horizontally below them all within safe bounds. The stamp hasn't entered yet but will appear shortly after.

The blur effect is working as intended—the three chips are noticeably soft while the counter text remains sharp and crisp. Now at t=20.10, the stamp enters from the left and its bottom edge lands at 353 pixels, leaving only 7 pixels before hitting the frame boundary. With the vertical drift applied, it could creep up to 364 pixels, which is dangerously close to clipping. I need to dial back the vertical offset at keyframe 11 to keep it safe.

Reducing the offset from 12 to 4 pixels lifts the stamp by about 13 pixels, bringing the bottom edge down to 340. The chip3 element shifts up accordingly but stays well within its 31-pixel margin. Actually, I should reduce the offset at both keyframe 10 and 11 to smooth out the motion—at keyframe 10 (16.55 seconds) the stamp isn't visible yet, but by 16.8 seconds it's fully in frame, so that's where the constraint kicks in. With the offset at 8 pixels at 16.55 and 4 at 20.10, chip3's top position shifts up by about 8 pixels, which is still safe.

Now checking the patch at 22.61 seconds: the counter and stamp are both dissolving at 0.13 opacity, and the focus transitions from chip2 to chip1 at this exact moment, so chip2 stays sharp while chip1 and chip3 get the blur effect. Chip3's position checks out at the right edge.

Moving forward to 27.10 seconds: chip1 is now in focus with zero blur, chip2 and chip3 are blurred at 1.45 and 2.91 respectively, and the station shifts focus to chip2. Then at 30.03 seconds, chip1 gets pushed further left to -831 with only a 29-pixel margin—very tight even accounting for the drift correction—while chip3 maintains a 33-pixel margin on the right.

This three-chip frame at cz=1752 is at the absolute limit of what works, so I'm backing off the zoom values slightly for safety: adjusting KF16 to 1726 and KF17 to 1734 instead of the current 1740 and 1752, which gives a bit more breathing room while still maintaining about 9% of the original compression.

The nod magnitude weakens from 1.2357 to 1.3079 (a 5.8% drop) with this adjustment, so I'm trying a compromise: reducing KF15's cz from 1668 to 1640 to preserve the nod strength at around 7.5% while keeping the tighter frame intact. I'm setting the keyframes to these new values and checking that all three chips stay cleanly within bounds at the next patch point.

At t=33.06, there's a timing problem: the camera's focus shifts to chip3, which is still off-frame at that moment, leaving both chip1 and chip2 blurred with nothing sharp visible until the camera travels left to bring chip3 into frame by t=36.00.

This creates roughly 1.5 seconds of "focus on nothing," which could work dramatically as an intentional search gesture—the narrator says "O terceiro..." and the camera whips to find it, everything blurs, then the third element arrives sharp. But the current swing is too slow at 2.94 seconds. I'm tightening it to 33.06 → 34.58 (46 frames, aligned with "controla"), which compresses the camera movement to about 16 pixels per frame and makes the reframe feel like a motivated, snappy gesture rather than a sluggish pan. me try a different approach: reduce the amplitude of the second move instead. Using dx=+150 at the chip1 beat gives x=−453 with a safe left position, but chip3 still clips at the right edge by about 35 pixels. I need to push chip3 further right, which means solving for the minimum dx value that keeps it fully in frame.

Working through the math: chip3's left edge needs to be at least 860 pixels, so (427+dx)×1.4412 ≥ 860, which gives dx ≥ 170. Using dx=+180 instead creates a swing from 180 to −250 over 68 frames—about 9.4 pixels per frame with a peak of 21 pixels per frame. It's brisk but works as a deliberate reframe, especially since the reference cuts rather than pans.

Now I'm verifying the collision zones with dx=180 at the chip1 beat. Chip1 clears its left third, chip2 fits in its zone, but chip3 only has a 9-pixel sliver of clearance—too tight. Pushing dx to 195 gives chip3 a proper 12-pixel margin while keeping chip1 and chip2 safe. Setting the keyframes to [31.91, 195, 45, 1738] for KF18 and adjusting the subsequent frames accordingly.

The third swing accelerates from 190 to -250 over 68 frames, peaking at 22 pixels per frame—the fastest motion in the sequence, justified by the camera searching for chip3. Then I hold the frame steady for 2 seconds while chip3 sits sharp and centered-right, which becomes the thesis moment. The patch at t=36.00 confirms chip3's bounds are within the safe range.

Now I'm checking the layout calculations for chip3's text at the scaled size—at 1.585x scale, the 15.5 layout pixels becomes about 15.4 pixels on phone, which is the best readability in the scene. But at t=41.00, there's an issue: the chips should be fading out between frames 40.20 and 41.10, and at exactly t=41.00 the opacity calculation shows they're nearly invisible at 0.037.

Let me reconsider the timing—the pull-back animation runs from 37.36 to 41.10 while the fade happens from 40.20 to 41.10, so there's a nice window from 37.36 to 40.20 where all three chips are visible and widening. I should verify the geometry at t=39.5 to confirm there's a clean three-chip frame before they start dissolving.

Checking the positions and scales at that moment: the three chips span horizontally from about -739 to 624, which looks good. Now I'm comparing the actual versus patched versions during the early phase to see if there's a defect in the left third positioning.

In the actual version, both host elements are tiny and centered, taking up only about 26% of the width and floating well inside the frame. The patch improves things by expanding the first element and cutting it properly at the bottom, but the second one still needs width adjustment to stop floating.

Now I'm applying a series of refinements to the keyframe values—adjusting vertical offsets, scaling factors, and positioning coordinates across multiple keyframes—then re-testing to see if the layout stabilizes.

At t=10.95 with PATCH, I'm checking the bounding boxes: host #0 spans from x=-455 to -233, host2 from 126 to 343, the mic from -211 to 26 horizontally, and the mug from 54 to 316. The mug is actually 262×262 while the mic is only 237×241, which means the caneca is materially larger—that's the visual defect confirmed. ## 🤖 Claude *(22:52:43)*

<details>
<summary>💭 Thinking Process</summary>

The scale mismatch is the real problem here — one presenter takes up about 79% of the frame while the other is only 65%, and the right one's floating with his shoes nowhere near the bottom while the left one's cut off at the ankles. They're supposed to be on the same plane but look like they're at completely different distances, which is the core defect: the width field is being ignored entirely, leaving both rendering at the same width despite the height values being set differently.

The white stroke edge treatment is also inconsistent — one PNG has it, the other doesn't, which means they weren't cut the same way. The horizon line shows up faintly around y+575 as a glow crossing behind both figures at hip height, which works as the floor reference I expected. The real issue with the spacing is that the figures only occupy the middle 45% of the width with empty space on both sides, and even with my camera adjustment bringing them from 26% to 44%, the layer positions at x=±430 can't span the 2.39:1 ratio needed at any compatible camera depth — the Encenador needs to be positioned around x=±860 instead.

Let me check the third stream frame quickly since that's where chip legibility should be at its peak, then I'll have enough to write this up.

</details>

## 🤖 Claude *(22:53:20)*

<details>
<summary>💭 Thinking Process</summary>

The layout has good focus separation with ÁUDIO sharp and prominent on the right while ADSENSE recedes softly to the left, but there's a major compositional issue — the chips occupy only the top quarter of the frame, leaving the bottom two-thirds completely empty.

I'm recalculating the vertical positioning constraints. At this keyframe (35.32), only the two chips are active, so I have much more freedom with the dy offset than I initially thought. The math shows dy can range from about 42 to 411 pixels, which means I could push the chips significantly lower. Setting dy around +215 would center the sharp chip element vertically or slightly above center — far better for a 2.39:1 aspect ratio than pinning it to the top edge.

This creates a substantial camera move from the previous keyframe: a 170-unit vertical crane combined with the 440-unit lateral shift, totaling about 707 pixels of screen movement over 68 frames. It's aggressive but intentional, and it reads as the camera deliberately reframing the scene rather than just settling into place.

The hierarchy works well here — the row starts as context while the number is the focus, then by the end the row becomes the subject and rises to center stage. For the final closing frame with all three chips wide and pulled back, I'm adjusting the vertical offset to around 150 to position them slightly above center rather than in the upper third, which gives better balance across the 2.39:1 aspect ratio.

Now I'm realizing the real problem: during the enumeration phase when only the chips are visible on screen, they're clustered in the top 15-30% of the frame, leaving 75% empty space below. That's a significant composition issue that's been there for six seconds. The vertical offset parameter is what controls this, so I need to raise it across the entire chip-only phase to better fill the frame.

Looking at the constraints during this window, I can push the chips higher without hitting any boundaries. Setting the offset to +190 would center the chips vertically around the middle of the frame, which actually creates a more balanced anamorphic composition with space distributed above and below rather than concentrated at the bottom.

But I need to be careful with the timing — the counter and stamp are still dissolving through this section, and if I jump the offset too aggressively, the counter block will drop below the safe zone and get clipped. So I'm staging the offset rise gradually across the keyframes: starting at 34 at KF12, ramping up through 60, 120, and 185 as the dissolve completes, then settling at 190 for the main enumeration phase, with a slight dip to 150 near the end.

At KF13 specifically, with an offset of 50, the stamp's bottom edge lands just at the boundary — barely visible at that opacity, which is acceptable. Let me verify this one more time to make sure the spacing holds throughout.

</details>

<details>

At t=30.03, the focus transition to chip1 (PATROCÍNIO) is just beginning, so the focus is still on chip2, which keeps ADSENSE sharp while PATROCÍNIO and ÁUDIO remain soft. The pull animation spans from 30.03 to 30.83. The three chips now stretch across the full width of the composition.

The frame composition works well — three chips spanning horizontally at mid-height with one sharp and two soft, anchored by the low horizon glow. The bottom third is still dark but the horizon provides visual grounding. However, the chips are quite small on mobile: the ADSENSE text measures around 12 physical pixels on a 1080-wide phone, which is marginal for readability. My measurements confirm the camera nearly doubled the apparent size (6.5 → 12 px), and Arte would need t1 at ~26px and t2 at ~18px to be comfortably legible. The focus separation between chips is now distinctly visible in this frame.

I'm satisfied with the composition and ready to finalize. I need to apply the dy 50→40 adjustment at KF13, then address the performance measurement. Since I can't run a proper A/B test with the host under load (whisper is at 10), I'll measure render time for a short burst on both versions back-to-back and report the ratio — that's what survives load variation. I'll grab 15 frames around the apex (t=16-17, the worst case for my patch) and compare the same frame indices across the full scene.

</details>

## 🤖 Claude *(22:56:50)*

<details>
<summary>💭 Thinking Process</summary>

Looking at the benchmark results from my A/B testing, the patch introduces a 15.3% performance overhead in render time—pushing from around 834ms to 961ms per frame under the current host load conditions. The ratio stays consistent across multiple runs, so while the absolute numbers are inflated due to the non-idle load state, the relative cost is reliable.

For the full scene render of 1233 frames, that translates to an extra 2.6 minutes compared to the baseline. The overhead comes from two places: the larger visual scale increases the raster area for every leaf element by about 4.9×, and the blur radius adjustment brings more leaves into the mid-range, adding computational work. I'm confident this trade-off is worth it—the patch delivers on the brief with better legibility, proper visual hierarchy, and the full set of camera events, so I'm documenting the cost explicitly and moving forward with the final tweaks. The cutouts rarely hit the blur clamp during actual interactions, so reducing maxBlur wouldn't yield meaningful savings. I'll keep it as is and instead apply the dy adjustment from 50 to 40 at keyframe 13. Now I'm structuring the output with my decision vocabulary — hold, push, pull, whip for motion types, easing functions, and focus-related tokens. I'm hitting some engine limitations that need workarounds: `cutout` only reads width and ignores height even though every score includes height values, layer intervals are locked to a single entry and exit so I can't make them re-enter, `chip.t2` lacks styling hooks that `t1` has so I can't scale sub-labels for mobile, the atmosphere system has fixed dimensions and plane constraints that won't stretch to 2.39:1 aspect ratio, and per-layer focus controls are incomplete.

I also need to handle cross-scene transitions since the global fade forces a dip-to-black that I can't override, and I'll document these gaps in the justification section with a few notes in decisions about what I'm deferring.

Let me verify the actual measurements I'm working with for the counter display — the current version shows "R$ 55.951.549" at around 531×71 pixels, and I'm targeting "R$ 100.000.000" at 1200×148 with a peak around 671×83.

Now I'm looking at the timing data: the counter hits its marks at 15.58 for "100" and 16.19 for "milhões", but the window was originally [15.34, 20.40] and now lands 4.21 seconds late, so I'm adjusting it to [15.34, 16.55]. The presenters are scaling from 122×336 up to 206×568, and I'm noticing the microphone at 237×241 renders smaller than the mug at 262×262. Performance-wise, I'm seeing a cost increase from 834 to 961 milliseconds per frame — about 15% slower across 1233 frames, adding roughly 2.6 minutes total. At the apex with focus k reduced from 95 to 55, the chips are carrying different blur amounts while the number stays at zero, and I'm checking phone legibility at around 6.5 pixels. So the glyph height on the phone comes out to around 44.6 physical pixels for ATUAL versus 93 for the probe measurement, which confirms the discrepancy I was tracking. Now I'm moving into analyzing the frames-per-onset table.

I'm converting the accent timing windows into frame counts at 30fps, which gives me a range from 24 frames for the fastest accent up to 112 frames for the longest one.
